Global Regular Solutions of the Compressible Navier-Stokes Equations with Nonlinear Density-Dependent Viscosities and Large Initial Data of Spherical Symmetry
Gui-Qiang G. Chen, Jiawen Zhang, Shengguo Zhu

Abstract
. For the physically important case in which the viscosity coefficients depend on the density ρ \rho through a power law ( i.e. , ρ δ \rho^{\delta} with some exponent δ ∈ ( 1 2 , 1 ) \delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1) ), we establish the global well-posedness of regular solutions of the compressible Navier-Stokes equations for barotropic flow with large initial data of spherical symmetry in two and three spatial dimensions. The initial density considered here is positive everywhere but vanishes in the far field, ensuring that the resulting solutions satisfy the conservation laws of total mass and momentum. The most crucial step in our analysis is to obtain a uniform upper bound for the density, which is challenging due to the combined difficulties of degeneracy near the far-field vacuum, coordinate singularity at the origin, and nonlinearity of viscosity coefficients. Furthermore, the methodology developed here can also be applied to the corresponding problem in which the density remains strictly away from the vacuum.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35Q30, 76N10, 35B65, 35A09.

1.
Introduction

The motion of viscous and Newtonian barotropic
fluids in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n=2
or
3
) is governed by the following compressible Navier-Stokes system (
CNS
):

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+\diver(\rho\boldsymbol{u})=0,\\[4.0pt]
\displaystyle(\rho\boldsymbol{u})_{t}+\diver(\rho\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u})+\nabla P=\diver\mathbb{T},\end{cases}

where
t\geq 0
denotes the time coordinate,
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the Eulerian spatial coordinate,
\rho\geq 0
the mass density of the fluid,
\boldsymbol{u}=(u_{1},\cdots\!,u_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the fluid velocity, and
P
the pressure of the fluid. For the polytropic gases, the constitutive relation is given by

P=A\rho^{\gamma},

where
A>0
is an entropy constant and
\gamma>1
is the adiabatic exponent. The viscous stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
is of the form:

(1.2)

\mathbb{T}=2\mu(\rho)D(\boldsymbol{u})+\lambda(\rho)\diver\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathbb{I}_{n},

where
D(\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{u})^{\top})
is the deformation tensor,
\mathbb{I}_{n}
is the
n\times n
identity matrix,

(1.3)

\mu(\rho)=a_{1}\rho^{\delta},\quad\lambda(\rho)=a_{2}\rho^{\delta},

for the viscosity exponent
\delta\geq 0
,
\mu(\rho)
is the shear viscosity coefficient,
\lambda(\rho)+\frac{2}{n}\mu(\rho)
is the bulk viscosity coefficient, and
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both constants satisfying

(1.4)

a_{1}>0,\qquad\ \ 2a_{1}+na_{2}\geq 0.

For rarefied gases, the full
CNS
can be formally derived from the Boltzmann equation
via the Chapman–Enskog expansion (see Chapman–Cowling
[
10
]
).
Under proper physical assumptions, the viscosity coefficients and the thermal conductivity coefficient are all functions
of the absolute temperature
\theta
. In fact,
for the cutoff inverse power force models, if the intermolecular potential varies as
\ell^{-\varkappa}
,
where
\ell
is the intermolecular distance and
\varkappa
is a positive constant, then

(1.5)

\mu(\theta)=b_{1}\theta^{\frac{1}{2}+b},\quad\lambda(\theta)=b_{2}\theta^{\frac{1}{2}+b},\quad\kappa(\theta)=b_{3}\theta^{\frac{1}{2}+b}\qquad\,\,\text{with $\,\,b=\frac{2}{\varkappa}\in[0,\infty)$},

for some constants
b_{i}
(
i=1,2,3
), where
\kappa(\theta)
is the thermal conductivity coefficient.
In particular,
\varkappa=1
for the ionized gas,
\varkappa=4
for Maxwellian molecules, and
\varkappa=\infty
for rigid elastic spherical molecules (see §10 of
[
10
]
). For polytropic fluids, such a dependence is inherited through the laws of Boyle and Gay-Lussac:

P=\hat{A}\rho\vartheta=A\rho^{\gamma}\qquad\text{for a constant }\hat{A}>0,

i.e.
,
\vartheta=A\hat{A}^{-1}\rho^{\gamma-1}
,
and the viscosity coefficients become functions of
\rho
taking form
(
1.3
)
.
Furthermore, there are other physical models satisfying the density-dependent viscosities assumption (
1.3
), including the Korteweg system, the shallow water equations, the quantum Navier-Stokes system, among others; see
[
3
,
20
,
23
]
and the references therein.

In this paper, we establish the global well-posedness of spherically symmetric (regular) solutions taking the form

\displaystyle(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})=(\rho(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|),u(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|})

of system
(
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n=2
or
3
) with the initial data:

(1.6)

(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(0,\boldsymbol{x})=(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})=(\rho_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|),u_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|})\qquad\text{for $\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$},

and the far-field behavior:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})\to(\bar{\rho},\boldsymbol{0})\qquad\text{as $|\boldsymbol{x}|\to\infty\,$ for $\,t\geq 0$},

where
\bar{\rho}\geq 0
is a constant.
Our results hold for all adiabatic exponents
\gamma\in(1,\infty)
and physical viscosity exponent
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and for
\gamma\in(1,6\delta-3)
and
\delta\in(7-2\sqrt{10},1)
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, without restriction on the size of the initial data. Moreover, the viscosity coefficients
(\mu(\rho),\lambda(\rho))
satisfy the Bresch–Desjardins (BD) relation (see
[
3
]
):

(1.8)

\lambda(\rho)=2(\rho\mu^{\prime}(\rho)-\mu(\rho)).

In particular, when
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.7
), our solutions have the conserved total mass and momentum, and the fluid density keeps positive in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
but decays to zero in the far-field, which is consistent with the facts that the total mass and momentum are conserved, and
CNS
is a model of non-dilute fluids.

When
(\mu,\lambda,\kappa)
are all constants, there is a vast literature on the well-posedness theory for
CNS
. In the absence of vacuum, the one-dimensional (1-D) problem has been studied extensively (see
[
31
,
33
,
34
,
60
]
and the references therein), and the local well-posedness of the multi-dimensional (M-D) classical solutions are known in
[
46
,
51
]
.
However, when vacuum appears, the approaches used in the references mentioned above do not work directly, owing to the degeneracy of the time evolution in the momentum equations, which makes it difficult to study the dynamics of the fluid velocity in the domain where the density vanishes.
In general, a vacuum is required for the far-field
under some physical requirements
such as finite total mass and total energy in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
By introducing some compatibility conditions, the local well-posedness of 3-D regular solutions with vacuum was established successfully
in Salvi–Straškraba
[
50
]
, Cho–Choe–Kim
[
15
]
, and the references therein.
On the other hand, it has been shown that, in the small data regime (near constant density), the M-D solutions remain globally regular if the initial data are smooth; see
[
18
,
43
]
and the references therein.
For M-D problems with general data, though many important results on the global existence of weak solutions have been obtained in
[
4
,
22
,
26
,
27
,
30
,
41
]
, the uniqueness problem
It is widely open due to the fairly low regularity of the solutions.

In fact, when
(\mu,\lambda,\kappa)
are all constants, no matter for the barotropic flow or the non-isentropic flow,
the global regularity of M-D solutions with large initial data remains open until now, even when the initial data exhibit some form of symmetry (if the domain considered contains the origin).
The main obstacle lies in obtaining uniform
a priori
bounds on the density, both above and below; see Lions
[
41
]
, Feireisl
[
22
]
, Sun–Wang–Zhang
[
54
]
, and the references therein.
For the M-D problem, as far as we know, such kind of bounds can only be obtained in the region excluding the origin for spherically symmetric flow; see
[
16
,
26
,
27
,
29
]
and the references therein.
Recently, for 3-D spherically symmetric flow, Merle–Raphaël–Rodnianski–Szeftel
[
44
]
proved that there exists a set of finite-energy smooth initial data with far-field vacuum for which the corresponding solutions to the barotropic
CNS
implode in a finite time
T
,
i.e.
,
for any
\varepsilon>0
,

(1.9)

\quad\ \ \ \ \lim_{t\to T}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{0})=\infty,\qquad\lim_{t\to T}\sup_{|\boldsymbol{x}|\leq\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|=\infty.

It is worth noting that the case
\gamma=\frac{5}{3}
, which corresponds to a monatomic gas, was excluded in
[
44
]

due to a triple-point degeneracy in the underlying analysis. This gap was later filled by Shao–Wang–Wei–Zhang
[
52
]
by introducing a novel renormalization
for some autonomous ODEs.
Moreover, for 3-D barotropic flow, in
[
7
]
, Buckmaster–Cao-Labora–Gómez-Serrano proved the finite-time blow-up of
CNS
with strictly positive initial density and spherical symmetry when
\gamma=\frac{7}{5}
(corresponding to a diatomic gas).
On the other hand, for 3-D barotropic
CNS
without symmetry assumption,
Cao-Labora–Gómez-Serrano–Shi–Staffilani
[
9
]
constructed some smooth solutions
that remain strictly away from vacuum and nevertheless develop an imploding finite-time
singularity in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

When vacuum appears,
some singular and counterintuitive behaviors of solutions to
CNS
with constant viscosity and thermal conductivity coefficients have been observed.
In particular, Hoff–Serre
[
28
]
showed that 1-D weak solutions need not depend continuously on their initial data when the initial density contains an interval of vacuum states, and Duan-Xin–Zhu
[
19
]
proved that the classical solutions with far-field vacuum cannot preserve the conservation of the momentum in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Such pathological phenomena may be traced back to the unphysical assumption that the viscosity coefficients are constants when modeling viscous fluids in the presence of vacuum, under which the vacuum exerts an artificial force on the fluid across the fluid–vacuum interface. Therefore, from a physical standpoint, compressible viscous flows near vacuum are therefore more appropriately modeled by the degenerate
CNS
with
(\mu(\theta),\lambda(\theta),\kappa(\theta))
shown in (
1.5
) for the nonisentropic flow or
(\mu(\rho),\lambda(\rho))
shown in (
1.3
) for the barotropic flow, respectively.

In fact, the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
)–(
1.4
) (with
\delta>0
in (
1.3
)) has received extensive attention in recent years.
Significant progress on the global existence of smooth solutions has been achieved when the initial density is strictly positive; see
[
17
,
25
,
32
,
45
]
for one-dimensional flows with general initial data, and
[
55
,
58
]
for two-dimensional flows with small initial data. Some significant progress has been made on the global existence of smooth solutions when the initial density is strictly positive; see
[
17
,
25
,
32
,
45
]
for 1-D flow with general data, and
[
55
,
58
]
for 2-D flow with small data.
However, when
\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})}=0
, the momentum equations become
degenerate in both the
time evolution and the spatial dissipation:

(1.10)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\rho(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u})}_{\circledast}+\nabla P=\underbrace{\diver(2\mu(\rho)D(\boldsymbol{u})+\lambda(\rho)\diver\boldsymbol{u}\mathbb{I}_{n})}_{\Diamond},

where
\circledast
denotes the degenerate time evolution, and
\Diamond
denotes the degenerate spatial dissipation.
Such a double degenerate structure shown in (
1.10
) makes it formidable to establish the propagation and mollification mechanisms of the regularity of solutions. A new mathematical entropy
function was first introduced in Bresch–Desjardins
[
3
]
for

(\mu(\rho),\lambda(\rho))
satisfying (
1.8
), which offers the well-known BD entropy estimate

(1.11)

\mu^{\prime}(\rho)\nabla\rho/\sqrt{\rho}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))

provided that
\mu^{\prime}(\rho_{0})\nabla\rho_{0}/\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
This observation plays a key role in the development of the global existence of M-D weak solutions with finite energy; see Bresch–Vasseur–Yu
[
5
]
, Li–Xin
[
37
]
, Vasseur–Yu
[
57
]
,
and the references therein.

Recently, based on some elaborate analysis of the intrinsic degenerate-singular structures of the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
)–(
1.4
), the local well-posedness of classical solutions with far-field vacuum is known in
[
11
,
38
,
39
,
59
]
and the references therein. In stark contrast with the case that the viscosity coefficients are constants, some solid progress have been obtained on the global regularity of solutions of the degenerate
CNS
with large initial data of spherical symmetry.
In particular, when
\delta=1
, by making full use of the linear dependence of the viscosity coefficient on the density,
Chen–Zhang–Zhu
[
13
]
have established global well-posedness of spherically symmetric smooth solutions in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n=2
or
3
) for the following two classes of initial density profiles:

(i)

the initial density
\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})
is strictly positive in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})>0;

(ii)

the initial density remains positive for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
but decays to zero in the far-field:

\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})>0\quad\text{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$},\qquad\quad\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\to 0\quad\text{as $|\boldsymbol{x}|\to\infty$}.

Furthermore, for the case
\delta=1
,
even when the initial density is compactly supported,
under the setting of the vacuum free boundary problem,
it is proved in Chen–Zhang–Zhu
[
14
]
that
the solutions with large initial data of spherical symmetry remain globally regular and unique in two and three spatial dimensions. Moreover, the physical vacuum boundary condition is allowed for the initial density profile considered in
[
14
]
, and the solutions are smooth all the way up to the moving vacuum boundary.

On the other hand, for the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
)–(
1.4
), when
\delta>1
, it is shown in
[
39
]
that, for certain classes of 3-D initial data with vacuum in some open set, one can construct corresponding local smooth solutions in the inhomogeneous Sobolev spaces, which break down in finite time, regardless of the size of the initial data. Moreover, in a very recent paper, for
\gamma\in(1,\,1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}})
except for at most countably many points,
Chen–Liu–Zhu
[
12
]
identified a value
\delta^{*}(\gamma)<\frac{1}{2}
,
i.e.
,

(1.12)

\displaystyle\delta^{*}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\gamma+1}{4}-\frac{\sqrt{2(\gamma-1)}}{2}\quad&\displaystyle\text{for}\,\,\,1<\gamma<\frac{5}{3},\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1-(2\sqrt{3}-3)\gamma}{2(3-\sqrt{3})}\quad&\displaystyle\text{for}\,\,\,\frac{5}{3}\leq\gamma<1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}},\end{cases}

such that,
for every
0<\delta<\delta^{*}(\gamma)
in (
1.3
),
there exists a class of smooth initial data for which the corresponding smooth solutions
implode in finite time at the origin (as shown in (
1.9
)) in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
(torus) or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
For ruling out
the possibility that the corresponding implosion is an artifact of the vacuum, the initial density profile considered in
[
12
]
is assumed to be strictly positive.

These observations shown in
[
7
,
9
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
39
,
44
,
52
]
for the global regularity of solutions with large data or finite time singularity formation indicate that the behavior of M-D regular solutions to
CNS
(
1.1
)–(
1.4
) depends sensitively on the viscosity exponent.
This strong dependence makes the analysis of global well-posedness for M-D regular solutions with general initial data highly intricate.

In this paper, for the physically important case
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, we study the global well-posedness of regular solutions of the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with large initial data of spherical symmetry in two and three spatial dimensions.
For this purpose, under assumption (
1.8
),
we first reformulate the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some time
T>0
into the following initial-boundary value problem (
IBVP
) in
\Omega_{T}:=[0,T]\times[0,\infty)
:

(1.13)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+u\rho_{r}+\rho\big(u_{r}+\frac{mu}{r}\big)=0,\\[3.0pt]
\displaystyle\rho u_{t}+\rho uu_{r}+A(\rho^{\gamma})_{r}=2a_{1}\delta\big(\rho^{\delta}u_{r}+\frac{m\rho^{\delta}u}{r}\big)_{r}-\frac{2a_{1}m(\rho^{\delta})_{r}u}{r},\\[7.0pt]
\displaystyle(\rho,u)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},u_{0})\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{for $r\in I:=[0,\infty)$},\\[7.0pt]
\displaystyle u|_{r=0}=0\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \qquad\text{for $t\in(0,T]$},\\[7.0pt]
\displaystyle(\rho,u)\to\left(0,0\right)\qquad\qquad\,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{as $r\to\infty$ \ for $t\in(0,T]$},\end{cases}

where
a_{1}>0
is a constant,
r=|\boldsymbol{x}|
,
m=n-1
, and the boundary condition
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{4}
is derived from the continuity of
\boldsymbol{x}\mapsto\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
at
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Drawing upon the progress presented in
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
39
]
, our results will further refine the global well-posedness theory of M-D smooth solutions with general data for the degenerate
CNS
.

The corresponding study on the global regularity of solutions with general data of (
1.13
) is extremely difficult, since the structure of momentum equation
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{2}
is full of degeneracy, singularity, and nonlinearity,
i.e.
,

(1.14)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\rho(u_{t}+uu_{r})}_{\circledast}+P_{r}-\underbrace{2a_{1}\delta(\rho^{\delta}u_{r})_{r}}_{\Diamond}-\underbrace{2a_{1}m\Big(\delta\big(\frac{\rho^{\delta}u}{r}\big)_{r}-\frac{(\rho^{\delta})_{r}u}{r}\Big)}_{\star}=0,

where
\star
denotes the coordinates singularity.
In fact, for the 1-D case (
m=n-1=0
),
the so-called coordinate singularity vanishes.
Hence, the analysis for M-D spherically symmetric flows is essentially different from that for 1-D flow.
Due to the compressibility of fluids,
we encounter the following two major obstacles:

•

possible cavitation,
i.e.
,
\rho(t,r)\to 0
for some
(t,r)\in(0,T]\times[0,\infty)
;

•

possible implosion,
i.e.
,
\rho(t,r)\to\infty
for some
(t,r)\in(0,T]\times[0,\infty)
.

Overcoming these difficulties is highly nontrivial because of three inherent issues:

•

the coordinate singularity at the origin, manifested by the singular factor
\tfrac{1}{r}
in equations
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{1}
–
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{2}
;

•

the degeneracies in both the time evolution (
\circledast
) and the spatial dissipation (
\Diamond
), arising from the far-field vacuum
in (
1.7
);

•

the strong nonlinearity in the spatial dissipation caused by the nonlinear density-dependent viscosity coefficients.

Thus, new ideas and techniques are required to establish the global well-posedness of spherical symmetric regular solutions
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
), especially when far-field vacuum appears.
Fortunately, in this paper, based on some elaborate analysis of the intrinsic degenerate-singular structures of (
1.1
) in the radial coordinates, we prove the global well-posedness of regular solutions of the degenerate
CNS
for barotropic flow
with large initial data of spherical symmetry and nonlinear density-dependent viscosities in two and three spatial dimensions. Our result holds without restrictions on the size of the initial data, and the solutions we obtained satisfy the conservation laws of total mass and momentum.
In our analysis, the most crucial step is to obtain the uniform upper bound of
\rho
, which is challenging due to the combined difficulties of degeneracy near the far-field vacuum, coordinate singularity at the origin, and nonlinearity of viscosity coefficients. Owing to the nonlinear dependence of the viscosity coefficients on the density (
i.e.
,
\delta\in(0,1)
in (
1.3
)) as well as the appearance of far-field vacuum, the dynamics of the fluid velocity is governed by an intrinsic
singular parabolic
system. To handle such a singular structure, we introduce well-designed singular weighted estimates for the fluid velocity. Besides, in the simpler case where the density is strictly positive, our method can be applied directly without major modification.

We now outline the organization of the rest of this paper.
In §
2
, the main theorems on the global well-posedness for spherically symmetric regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) are presented.
In §
3
, we introduce the notations, present an enlarged reformulation of (
1.1
), and then outline the main strategies of our analysis.
In §
4
–§
8
, we provide a detailed proof of the global well-posedness under the far-field vacuum case stated in §
2
.
On one hand, in §
4
–§
7
,
we establish the global uniform estimates for the regular solutions in carefully designed function spaces, which consist of:

(i)

The global
a priori
upper bound for
\rho
(§
4
);

(ii)

The global
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-estimate for the effective velocity (§
5
);

(iii)

The non-formation of vacuum inside the fluids in finite time (§
6
);

(iv)

The global singular weighted estimates for regular solutions (§
7
).

On the other hand, based on these uniform estimates, in §
8
, we obtain the desired global well-posedness
of regular solutions,
by using the method of continuity. In §
9
, we sketch the proof for the global well-posedness of regular solutions with strictly positive initial density. Furthermore, we briefly outline the proof for the desired local well-posedness in §
10
. Finally, we list some auxiliary lemmas and new Sobolev embedding theorems
for spherically symmetric functions that are used frequently throughout this paper
in Appendices
A
–
C
.

2.
Main Theorems

This section is devoted to stating our main theorems on the global well-posedness of the spherically symmetric regular solutions
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) for general data in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2,3
. For simplicity, throughout this paper,
for any function space defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the following conventions are used for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
unless otherwise specified:

(2.1)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\|f\|_{H^{k}}=\|f\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\|f\|_{W^{k,p}}=\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

\displaystyle D^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\big\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\,\|f\|_{D^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty\big\},

\displaystyle D^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=D^{k,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\|f\|_{D^{k,p}}=\|f\|_{D^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\|f\|_{D^{k}}=\|f\|_{D^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

\displaystyle H^{-k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=(H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))^{*},\quad\|(f,g)\|_{X}=\|f\|_{X}+\|g\|_{X}.

2.1.
Global Spherically Symmetric Solutions of the Degenerate
CNS
with Far-Field Vacuum

We first address the case that
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.7
). Consider the following physical range of
(\delta,\gamma)
in system (
1.1
):

(2.2)

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\in(\frac{1}{2},1),\quad

\displaystyle\gamma\in(1,\infty)\ \quad

\displaystyle\text{if $n=2$},

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\in(7-2\sqrt{10},1),\quad

\displaystyle\gamma\in(1,6\delta-3)\ \quad

\displaystyle\text{if $n=3$}.

Notice that, if
\rho>0
, the momentum equations
\eqref{eq:1.1}_{2}
can be formally rewritten as

(2.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\nabla\rho^{\gamma-1}+\rho^{\delta-1}L\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{\delta}{\delta-1}\nabla\rho^{\delta-1}\cdot Q(\boldsymbol{u}),

where
L\boldsymbol{u}
and
Q(\boldsymbol{u})
are given by

(2.4)

L\boldsymbol{u}=-a_{1}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-(a_{1}+a_{2})\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{u},\qquad Q(\boldsymbol{u})=2a_{1}D(\boldsymbol{u})+a_{2}\diver\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathbb{I}_{n}.

We find that in (
2.3
), the degeneracies both in the time evolution and spatial dissipation have been transferred to the possible singularities of
(\nabla\rho^{\delta-1},\rho^{\delta-1}L\boldsymbol{u})
.

It is worth pointing out that, when vacuum appears in the far field, the dynamics of the velocity field
u
is governed by the quasilinear singular parabolic system (
2.3
)–(
2.4
). Compared with the case
\delta=1
studied in
[
8
,
13
,
38
]
,
some new obstacles for the global well-posedness theory with large initial data and far field vacuum arise:

(i)

The source term on the right-hand side of (
2.3
) contains some singularity as

\nabla\rho^{\delta-1}=(\delta-1)\rho^{\delta-1}\nabla\log\rho,

whose behavior is more singular than that of
\nabla\log\rho
in
[
8
,
13
,
38
]
due to
\delta-1<0
when
\rho\to 0
as
|\boldsymbol{x}|\to\infty
;

(ii)

The coefficient
\rho^{\delta-1}
in front of the Lamé operator
L\boldsymbol{u}
tends to
\infty
as
\rho\to 0
in the far filed instead of equaling to
1
in
[
8
,
13
,
38
]
. Then it is necessary to show that the term
\rho^{\delta-1}Lu
is well defined in some smooth function space.

Therefore, the two quantities

(\rho^{\gamma-1},\nabla\rho^{\delta-1})

will play significant roles in our analysis on the high-order regularity of the fluid velocity

\boldsymbol{u}
.
Based on the above observation, we first introduce a proper class of solutions, called regular solutions, of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
).

Definition 2.1
.

The vector function
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is called a regular solution of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n=2
or
3)
for
T>0
if
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
satisfy the following properties
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i)}

\displaystyle(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})\ \text{satisfies this problem in the sense of distributions};

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii)}

\displaystyle 0<\rho\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\rho^{\gamma-1}\in C([0,T];H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\displaystyle\nabla\rho^{\delta-1}\in C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \nabla\rho^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\in C([0,T];L^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{n}));

\displaystyle\mathrm{(iii)}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \rho^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\rho^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\rho^{\delta-1}\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\rho^{\delta-1}\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\rho^{\delta-1}\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).

Remark 2.1
.

We first introduce some physical quantities to be used in this paper
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle(\textrm{total mass}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\rho\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle(\textrm{momentum}).

It will be shown later that the regular solutions defined here satisfy the conservation of
\mathcal{M}(t)
and
\mathcal{P}(t)
. Next, the regularity of
\rho
shown in
Definition
2.1

implies that
\rho>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
but decays to zero
in the far-field,
which is consistent with the facts
that
\mathcal{M}(t)
and
\mathcal{P}(t)
are both conserved
and
CNS
is a model of non-dilute fluids.
Thus, the definition of regular solutions is consistent with the physical background of
CNS
.

Our main theorem is on the global well-posedness of the spherically symmetric regular solutions
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) for general initial data. The regularity of this solution ensures uniqueness in both the 2-D and 3-D cases and demonstrates that it is a classical solution of this problem.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
n=2
or
3
,
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.7
), and (
1.8
) and (
2.2
) hold. Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric and satisfy

(2.5)

\displaystyle 0<\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\qquad\nabla\rho_{0}^{\delta-1}\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\qquad\nabla\rho_{0}^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\in L^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\displaystyle\nabla\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

and the initial compatibility conditions

(2.6)

\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=\rho^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{1},\qquad\nabla(\rho^{\delta-1}_{0}L\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\rho^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{2},\qquad L\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=\rho^{1-\delta}_{0}\boldsymbol{g}_{*},

for some matrices
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a vector function
\boldsymbol{g}_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)
–
(
1.7
) admits a unique global regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfies

(2.7)

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];D^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).

Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with the form
:

(2.8)

(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})=(\rho(t,r),u(t,r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r})\qquad\text{for $r=|\boldsymbol{x}|$}

and satisfies the following properties
:

\mathrm{(i)}

The solution we obtain here
is a classical solution
:

(2.9)

\displaystyle(\rho,\nabla\rho,\rho_{t},\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{u})\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),\qquad(\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}).

\mathrm{(ii)}

The conservations of total mass and total momentum
(
that remains zero
)
hold
:

(2.10)

\mathcal{M}(t)=\mathcal{M}(0),\quad\,\mathcal{P}(t)\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\ \text{for $t\in[0,T]$};

\mathrm{(iii)}

For any
T>0
and
(t,r)\in(0,T]\times[0,\infty)
,

(2.11)

\begin{gathered}\frac{C(T)^{-1}\underline{\rho}(r)}{(r^{\frac{1}{2-2\delta}}+1)(\underline{\rho}(r)+1)}\leq\rho(t,r)\leq\min\big\{C(T),Cr^{-n+1}\big\},\end{gathered}

where
\underline{\rho}(r):=\inf_{z\in[0,r]}\rho_{0}(z)
,

C\geq 1
is a constant depending only on
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
and
(n,a_{1},\delta,\gamma,A)
, and
C(T)\geq 1
is a constant depending only on
(C,T)
.

As a direct consequence of Lemma
A.10
in Appendix
A
, the initial assumption (
2.5
) in Theorem
2.1
can be simplified when
\gamma\geq\delta+\frac{1}{2}
.

Corollary 2.1
.

If the constraint on
(\delta,\gamma)
in (
2.2
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\in(\frac{1}{2},1),\quad

\displaystyle\gamma\in\big[\delta+\frac{1}{2},\infty\big)\ \quad

\displaystyle\text{if $n=2$},

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\in(\frac{7}{10},1),\quad

\displaystyle\gamma\in\big[\delta+\frac{1}{2},6\delta-3\big)\ \quad

\displaystyle\text{if $n=3$},

then the initial condition (
2.5
) in
Theorem
2.1
can be reduced to

0<\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\rho_{0}^{\delta-1}\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\rho_{0}^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\in L^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

We now make some remarks on the results of this paper.

Remark 2.2
.

On one hand, the constraints on the viscosity coefficients given in (
1.4
) and (
1.8
) automatically imply

\delta\geq 1-\frac{1}{n}.

On the other hand, for technical reasons, in deriving the global uniform upper bound of density
\rho
in
§
4
, a crucial step is to establish the
L^{p}(I)
-energy estimates with some
p>n
for both the velocity and the effective velocity. This requires

(2.12)

\delta>\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{when $n=2$},\qquad\delta>7-2\sqrt{10}\,(\approx 0.675>\frac{2}{3})\quad\text{when $n=3$}.

Therefore, since we focus on the global well-posedness for
CNS
(
1.1
) when
\delta\in(0,1)
, the conditions of
\delta
given in (
2.2
) are optimal under our methodology.

Remark 2.3
.

The initial conditions (
2.5
)–(
2.6
) required in
Theorem
2.1
identify a general class of spherically symmetric initial data, which ensures the unique solvability of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
). For example, one can choose
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
satisfying the following constraints
:

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=u_{0}(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
and
0<\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

with
\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=\rho_{0}(r)
such that

0<\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{\sigma}\rho_{0}(r)<\infty\qquad\text{for $\ \max\{n,\,\frac{n-2}{2\gamma-2}\}<\sigma<\frac{1}{1-\delta}$}.

Remark 2.4
.

We give some comments on the constraints for
\rho_{0}
given
in
Theorem
2.1
.
On one hand, it follows from (
2.5
)–(
2.6
) and
Lemma
A.10
that
\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

On the other hand, the boundedness of the effective velocity

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{2a_{1}\delta}{\delta-1}\nabla\rho^{\delta-1}
plays a key role in our analysis,
which requires that
\boldsymbol{v}(0,\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Since
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
holds by classical Sobolev embedding theorems, we still need

\nabla\rho_{0}^{\delta-1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This follows directly from
\nabla\rho_{0}^{\delta-1}\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in (
2.5
) due to the critical Sobolev embedding
D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for spherically symmetric vector functions
(
see
Lemma
C.2

in
Appendix
C
)
.

Remark 2.5
.

The distinction of the range of
\gamma
for the
2
-
D
case and
3
-
D
case stems from the factor
r^{\frac{m}{2}}

(m=n-1)
in the BD entropy estimates under the spherical coordinates
(
see
Lemma
4.2
in
§
4
)
:

(2.13)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|r^{\frac{m}{2}}(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}\big\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq C_{0},

where
I=[0,\infty)
and
C_{0}>0
is a constant depending only on
(\rho_{0},u_{0},a_{1},\delta,\gamma,A,n)
. Notably, (
2.13
) is some
r
-weighted
L^{2}(I)
-estimate of
(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}
,
and the power of
r
in the
3
-
D
case is larger than
that in the
2
-
D
case.

In fact, in
§
4
, a crucial step in establishing the global uniform upper bound for
\rho
is to derive some new
r
-weighted estimates for
\rho
near the origin via the Hardy inequality
(
see
Lemma
A.4
in
Appendix
A
)
and the BD entropy estimates (
2.13
)
:

(2.14)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|r^{K}\rho\|_{L^{p}(0,1)}\leq C\qquad\,\text{for $p\in[1,\infty]$},

where
C>0
is a constant depending on
(C_{0},p,K)
, and exponent
K
satisfies

K>-\frac{1}{p}\quad\text{if $n=2$},\qquad K\geq\frac{1}{2\delta-1}-\frac{1}{p}\quad\text{if $n=3$}.

Clearly, the range of
K
is smaller when
n=3
, and this is main reason that narrows the admissible range of
\gamma
in this case. More detailed calculations can be found in
§
4.2
.

2.2.
Global Spherically Symmetric Solutions of the Degenerate
CNS
with Strictly Positive Initial Density

As a direct application of our approach, we can establish the global well-posedness of regular solutions with general data when
\bar{\rho}>0
in (
1.7
). We first define the corresponding regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
).

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\bar{\rho}>0
in (
1.7
) and
T>0
.
The vector function
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is called a regular solution of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)
–
(
1.7
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n=2
or
3)
if
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
satisfy the following properties
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i)}

\displaystyle(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})\ \text{satisfies this problem in the sense of distributions};

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii)}

\displaystyle\inf_{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})>0,\quad\rho-\bar{\rho}\in C([0,T];H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}));

\displaystyle\mathrm{(iii)}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).

When the initial density is strictly positive, our main result on the global well-posedness
of the regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) is stated as follows:

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
n=2
or
3
,
\bar{\rho}>0
in (
1.7
), and
(
1.8
) and(
2.2
) hold. Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric and satisfy

(2.15)

\displaystyle\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})>0,\qquad(\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)
–
(
1.7
) admits a unique global regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfies (
2.7
), (
2.9
), and

(2.16)

C(T)^{-1}\leq\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq C(T)\qquad\text{for all $(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$},

where
C(T)\geq 1
is a constant depending only on
(T,\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},n,a_{1},\delta,\gamma,A)
. Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.8
).

3.
Notations, Reformulations, and Main Strategies

In this section, we first present some notations and conventions in §
3.1
, which are frequently used throughout this paper. Next, in §
3.2
, we introduce an enlarged reformulation for the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
) to deal with the degeneracy caused by the far-field vacuum. In §
3.3
, based on such a reformulation, we show the main strategy and new ideas in our analysis.

3.1.
Notations

Throughout the rest of this paper,
unless otherwise specified, we adopt the notations in (
2.1
) and the following ones.

3.1.1.
Notations in M-D Eulerian coordinates

•

We always let
n=2
or
3
be the dimension of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and denote
m:=n-1
.

•

For a variable
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
(
l\geq 2
), its
i
-th component is denoted by
y_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq l
), and
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\cdots\!,y_{l})^{\top}
. We always let
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}
be the spatial variable of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

For any vector function
\boldsymbol{f}:E\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
(
l,q\geq 2
,
E
is a measurable set), its
i
-th component is denoted by
f_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq q
), and
\boldsymbol{f}=(f_{1},\cdots\!,f_{q})^{\top}
.

•

For any function
f
defined on a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
(
l\geq 1
), if the independent variable of
f
is
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\cdots\!,y_{l})^{\top}
, then

\displaystyle\qquad\partial_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}f=\partial_{y_{1}}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial_{y_{l}}^{\varsigma_{l}}f=f_{\underbrace{\text{\tiny$y_{1}\cdots y_{1}$}}_{\text{$\varsigma_{1}$-times}}\cdots\underbrace{\text{\tiny$y_{l}\cdots y_{l}$}}_{\text{$\varsigma_{l}$-times}}}=\frac{\partial^{\varsigma_{1}+\cdots+\varsigma_{l}}}{\partial y_{1}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial y_{l}^{\varsigma_{l}}}f\quad\ \text{for }\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=(\varsigma_{1},\cdots\!,\varsigma_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{l},

\displaystyle\qquad\nabla_{\boldsymbol{y}}f=(\partial_{y_{1}}f,\cdots\!,\partial_{y_{l}}f)^{\top},\qquad\Delta_{\boldsymbol{y}}f=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\partial_{y_{i}}^{2}f,

\displaystyle\qquad\nabla_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}f\text{ denotes one generic }\partial_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}f\text{ with }|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}|=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\varsigma_{i}=k\text{ for integer }k\geq 2,

\displaystyle\qquad|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}f|=\Big(\sum_{|\varsigma|=k}|\partial_{y_{1}}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial^{\varsigma_{l}}_{y_{l}}f|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{ for }k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

In particular, for the derivatives with respect to the
variable
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we use the notations

(\partial_{i}^{\varsigma_{i}},\partial^{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}},\nabla,\Delta,\nabla^{k})=(\partial_{x_{i}}^{\varsigma_{i}},\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}},\Delta_{\boldsymbol{x}},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{k})
.

•

If
\boldsymbol{f}:E\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
(
l,q\geq 2
,
E
is a measurable set) is a vector function with the independent variable
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\cdots\!,y_{l})^{\top}
and
X\in\{\partial_{y_{i}}^{\varsigma_{i}},\partial_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\varsigma},\Delta_{\boldsymbol{y}},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}\}
, then

\displaystyle X\boldsymbol{f}=\big(Xf_{1},\cdots\!,Xf_{q}\big)^{\top},\ \quad\nabla_{\boldsymbol{y}}\boldsymbol{f}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{y_{1}}f_{1}&\partial_{y_{2}}f_{1}&\cdots&\partial_{y_{l}}f_{1}\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{y_{1}}f_{2}&\partial_{y_{2}}f_{2}&\cdots&\partial_{y_{l}}f_{2}\\[5.69054pt]
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{y_{1}}f_{q}&\partial_{y_{2}}f_{q}&\cdots&\partial_{y_{l}}f_{q}\end{pmatrix}_{q\times l},

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{k}\boldsymbol{f}|=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{|\varsigma|=k}\big|\partial_{y_{1}}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial^{\varsigma_{l}}_{y_{l}}f_{i}\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{ for }k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

Moreover, if
l=j+i
with
j\geq 0
and the independent variable
\boldsymbol{y}
takes the form
\boldsymbol{y}=(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{\tilde{y}})^{\top}
with
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\cdots\!,s_{j})^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{\tilde{y}}=(\tilde{y}_{1},\cdots\!,\tilde{y}_{i})^{\top}
, then

\diver_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{y}}}\boldsymbol{f}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}\partial_{\tilde{y}_{k}}f_{k}.

In particular, if
j=0,1
,
\,i=n
, and
\boldsymbol{\tilde{y}}=\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
\diver=\diver_{\boldsymbol{x}}
.

3.1.2.
Notations in M-D spherical coordinates

•

We always let
r=|\boldsymbol{x}|
be the radial distance in spherical coordinates, and let
r\in I:=[0,\infty)
. Moreover, the following conventions are adapted:

\displaystyle|f|_{p}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(I)},\quad\|f\|_{k}=\|f\|_{H^{k}(I)},\quad\|f\|_{k,p}=\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(I)}\qquad\,\,\text{for $k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}$}.

In particular, for the Sobolev spaces defined on the open interval
(a,b)\subset I
, we use the abbreviation
X(a,b)=X((a,b))
for
X=L^{p}
,
W^{k,p}
, and
H^{k}
.

•

To simplify the notations, in the rest of the paper, we define the operator
\mathrm{D}_{r}
as

\qquad\begin{aligned} \mathrm{D}_{r}f&:=(f_{r},\frac{f}{r}),\qquad\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}f:=(f_{rr},(\frac{f}{r})_{r}),\\
\mathrm{D}_{r}^{3}f&:=(f_{rrr},\frac{f_{rr}}{r},(\frac{f}{r})_{rr},\frac{1}{r}(\frac{f}{r})_{r}),\qquad\mathrm{D}_{r}^{4}f:=(f_{rrrr},(\frac{f_{rr}}{r})_{r},(\frac{f}{r})_{rrr},(\frac{1}{r}(\frac{f}{r})_{r})_{r}).\end{aligned}

Notice from Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B
that the operator
\mathrm{D}_{r}
can be formally seen as a representative of the gradient
\nabla
in the radial coordinate. Besides, the following useful properties will be used in later analysis: for
f=f(t,r)
,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{D}_{r}f)_{r}=\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}f,\qquad(\mathrm{D}_{r}^{3}f)_{r}=\mathrm{D}_{r}^{4}f,\qquad\mathrm{D}_{r}^{j}f_{t}=(\mathrm{D}_{r}^{j}f)_{t}\quad\text{for $j=1,2,3,4$},

\displaystyle|f_{r}|^{2}+\Big|\frac{f}{r}\Big|^{2}=|\mathrm{D}_{r}f|^{2},\qquad|f_{rr}|^{2}+\Big|(\frac{f}{r})_{r}\Big|^{2}=|\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}f|^{2},

\displaystyle|(\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}f)_{r}|^{2}+\Big|\frac{\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}f}{r}\Big|^{2}=|\mathrm{D}_{r}^{3}f|^{2},\qquad|(\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}f)_{rr}|^{2}+\Big|\big(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|^{2}=|\mathrm{D}_{r}^{4}f|^{2}.

3.1.3.
Other notations and conventions

•

C^{\ell}(\overline{\Omega})

(\ell\in\mathbb{N},\,C(\overline{\Omega})=C^{0}(\overline{\Omega}))

denotes the space of all functions
f
for which
\nabla^{j}f

(0\leq j\leq\ell)
is bounded and uniformly continuous in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is equipped with the norm:

\|f\|_{C^{\ell}(\overline{\Omega})}:=\max_{0\leq j\leq\ell}\|\nabla^{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

In particular, if
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
C^{\ell}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
denotes the space of all functions
f
for which
\nabla^{j}f

(0\leq j\leq\ell)
is bounded and uniformly continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\Omega)
denotes the space of all functions
f
for which
\nabla^{j}f

(j\in\mathbb{N})
is continuous and compactly supported in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

For any function spaces
(X,X_{1},\cdots\!,X_{k})
and functions
(h,f,f_{1},\cdots\!,f_{k})
,

\|f\|_{X_{1}\cap\cdots\cap X_{k}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|f\|_{X_{i}},\qquad\|h(f_{1},\cdots\!,f_{k})\|_{X}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|hf_{i}\|_{X}.

•

For any
n\times n
real matrix
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}_{ij}
denotes its
(i,j)
-th entry.
\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{N}:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{ij}\mathcal{N}_{ij}
for any
n\times n
matrices
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
. Moreover,
\mathrm{SO}(n)
denotes the set of all
n\times n
real orthogonal matrices
\mathcal{O}
such that
\det\mathcal{O}=1
, where
\det\mathcal{O}
is the determinant of
\mathcal{O}
.

•

\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol satisfying
\delta_{ij}=1
when
i=j
,
and
\delta_{ij}=0
otherwise.

•

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X^{*}\times X}
denotes the pairing between the space
X
and its dual space
X^{*}
.

•

To integrate the estimates for both the
2
-D case and
3
-D case, we introduce the following parameter
n^{*}
:

n^{*}:=\frac{2n}{n-2}=\begin{cases}\infty&\quad\text{if $n=2$},\\
6&\quad\text{if $n=3$}.\end{cases}

3.2.
An Enlarged Reformulation

We first make an enlarged reformulation for the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
). In terms of variables

(3.1)

\phi=\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma-1},\qquad\boldsymbol{\psi}=\frac{\delta}{\delta-1}\nabla\rho^{\delta-1}=\frac{\delta}{\delta-1}\big(\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\big)^{\frac{1-\delta}{\gamma-1}}\nabla\phi^{\frac{\delta-1}{\gamma-1}}=(\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3})^{\top},

and
\boldsymbol{u}
, system (
1.1
) can be rewritten as

(3.2)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\phi_{t}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\phi+(\gamma-1)\phi\diver\boldsymbol{u}=0,\\[4.0pt]
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla\phi+a\phi^{2\iota}L\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot Q(\boldsymbol{u}),\\[4.0pt]
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})+(\delta-1)\boldsymbol{\psi}\diver\boldsymbol{u}+\delta a\phi^{2\iota}\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0},\end{cases}

where
L\boldsymbol{u}
and
Q(\boldsymbol{u})
are defined in (
2.4
), and

(3.3)

\displaystyle a=\big(\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\big)^{\frac{1-\delta}{\gamma-1}}\qquad\text{and}\quad\iota=\frac{\delta-1}{2(\gamma-1)}<0.

We aim to establish global spherically symmetric solutions of (3.2) with the form:

(3.4)

(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})(t,\boldsymbol{x})=(\phi(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|),u(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|},\psi(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|}),

with the initial data:

(3.5)

\displaystyle(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\left(\phi_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}\right)(\boldsymbol{x}):=(\phi_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|),u_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|},\frac{a\delta}{\delta-1}\nabla\phi_{0}^{2\iota}(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|})

for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the far-field asymptotic condition:

(3.6)

(\phi,\boldsymbol{u})\to(0,\boldsymbol{0})\qquad\text{as $|\boldsymbol{x}|\to\infty$ \ for $t\geq 0$}.

Then we define the corresponding regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
3.2
)–(
3.6
).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
T>0
. The vector function
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})
is called a regular solution of the Cauchy problem (
3.2
)
–
(
3.6
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n=2
or
3)
if
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})
satisfy the following properties
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i)}

\displaystyle(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})\ \text{satisfies this problem in the sense of distributions};

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii)}

\displaystyle 0<\phi^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\phi\in C([0,T];D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \nabla\phi^{\iota}\in C([0,T];L^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{n}));

\displaystyle\mathrm{(iii)}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \phi^{\iota}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\phi^{\iota}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\phi^{2\iota}\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\phi^{2\iota}\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\phi^{2\iota}\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).

Since
\partial_{x_{i}}\psi_{j}=\partial_{x_{j}}\psi_{i}
(
i,j=1,\cdots\!,n
), equations
\eqref{eq:cccq}_{3}
can be rewritten as

(3.7)

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{l}\partial_{l}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\delta a\phi^{2\iota}\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0},

where
\mathcal{A}_{l}=(a^{l}_{ij})_{n\times n}
for
i,j,l=1,\cdots\!,n
,
are symmetric matrices with
a^{l}_{ij}=u_{l}
for
i=j
and otherwise
a^{l}_{ij}=0
, and
\mathcal{B}=(\nabla u)^{\top}+(\delta-1)\diver u\,\mathbb{I}_{n}
. This implies that the source term
\boldsymbol{\psi}
can be controlled by the symmetric hyperbolic system (
3.7
).

Note that the enlarged system (
3.2
) consists of (up to leading order)

•

scalar transport
equation
\eqref{eq:cccq}_{1}
for
\phi
;

•

singular parabolic
system
\eqref{eq:cccq}_{2}
for the velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
;

•

symmetric hyperbolic
system
\eqref{eq:cccq}_{4}
but with several singular source terms for
\boldsymbol{\psi}
,

such a structure enables us to establish the following local well-posedness of the Cauchy problem (
3.2
)–(
3.6
).

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
n=2
or
3
, and let
(\delta,\gamma)
satisfy

(3.8)

\gamma\in(1,\infty),\qquad\delta\in(1-\frac{1}{n},1).

Assume that the initial data
(\phi_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0})
satisfy

(3.9)

\displaystyle 0<\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\phi_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{0}^{\iota}\in L^{2n}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

and the initial compatibility conditions

(3.10)

\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=\phi^{-\iota}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{1},\qquad\nabla(\phi^{2\iota}_{0}L\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\phi^{-\iota}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{2},\qquad L\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=\phi^{-2\iota}_{0}\boldsymbol{g}_{*},

for some matrices
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a vector function
\boldsymbol{g}_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then there exists
T_{*}>0
such that the Cauchy problem (
3.2
)
–
(
3.6
) admits a unique regular solution
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.7
) with
T
replaced by
T_{*}
, and

(3.11)

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\frac{a\delta}{\delta-1}\nabla\phi^{2\iota}\qquad\text{for {a.e.} $(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T_{*})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

Moreover,
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})
is spherically symmetric with form (
3.4
).

The proofs for Theorem
3.1
are given in §
10
. Moreover, at the end of §
10
, we show that Theorem
3.9
indeed implies the local well-posedness of regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) and (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) with general smooth, spherically symmetric initidata and far-field vacuum (
i.e.
,
\bar{\rho}=0
), which are stated in Theorem
3.2
below.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
n=2
or
3
, and (
3.8
) hold. If the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric, and satisfy (
2.5
)–(
2.6
), then there exists
T_{*}>0
such that the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)
–
(
1.7
) admits a unique regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.7
) with
T
replaced by
T_{*}
, and

(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{tt}\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T_{*}];D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad(\nabla\rho^{\delta-1})_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).

Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.8
).

3.3.
Main Strategies

We outline the proof for the global well-posedness of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
(
1.6
)–(
1.8
), including the global upper bound of the density, the
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of the effective velocity, the non-formation of vacuum inside the fluids, and the uniform estimates for the regular solutions. For brevity, we only sketch the proof for the
3
-D case; the
2
-D case can be treated in a similar way.

3.3.1.
Global uniform upper bound of
\rho

The first major task is to obtain the global uniform upper bound of density
\rho
. Due to the coordinate singularity at the origin, we divide the proof into two parts: the interior region
B_{1}=\{\boldsymbol{x}:\,|\boldsymbol{x}|<1\}
and the exterior region
\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash B_{1}
.

In the exterior domain, by the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}(1,\infty)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(1,\infty)
, the conservation of total mass, and the BD entropy estimates (see Lemma
4.2
), we obtain

(3.12)

\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times[1,\infty))}\leq C_{0}.

In the interior region
B_{1}
, the analysis is more subtle due to the geometric singularity. To overcome this difficulty, we introduce the effective velocity
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{2a_{1}\delta}{\delta-1}\nabla\rho^{\delta-1}
(its radial projection is
v=u+\frac{2a_{1}\delta}{\delta-1}(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}
) and employ a combination of the Hardy inequality and weighted estimates for
\rho
. First, due to
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
p>3
, we observe

(3.13)

\displaystyle\|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}+\delta-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}+\delta-1}\|_{W^{1,p}(B_{1})}\leq C(p)\|\rho\|_{L^{p\delta-p+1}(B_{1})}^{\delta-1+\frac{1}{p}}+C(p)\big\|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)\big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\|r^{\frac{2}{p\delta-p+1}}\rho\|_{L^{p\delta-p+1}(0,1)}^{\delta-1+\frac{1}{p}}+C(p)\big|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)\big|_{p}:=J_{*}+J_{**}.

To control the right-hand side of (
3.13
), we derive
r
-weighted
L^{q}
-estimates for
\rho
via the Hardy inequality and the BD entropy estimate
\nabla\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
:

(3.14)

\big\|r^{\frac{1}{2\delta-1}-\frac{1}{q}}\rho\big\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{q}(0,1))}\leq C(q)\qquad\text{for $q\in[2\delta-1,\infty]$}.

Then, if
p\in(3,\frac{1}{1-\delta})
(since
\delta>\frac{2}{3}
),
J_{*}
can be controlled by using the Hölder inequality and (
3.14
). To estimate
J_{**}
, we establish the
L^{p}(I)
-energy estimates for both
u
and
v
via multiplying
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{2}
and equation (
4.14
) by
r^{2}|u|^{p-2}u
and
r^{2}\rho|v|^{p-2}v
, respectively. By handling the exterior part via (
3.12
), and treating the interior part via (
3.14
) and an iterative scheme involving the Hardy inequality, we arrive at the estimates of the form:

(3.15)

\big\|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)\big\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{p}(I))}+\big\|(r^{2}\rho^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}|u|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u\big\|_{L^{p}([0,T];L^{p}(I))}\leq C(p,T)

for any
p\in[2,\tilde{p}(\delta))
. Here,
\tilde{p}(\delta)
is a parameter that depends only on
\delta
, which is strictly larger than
3
whenever
\delta>7-2\sqrt{10}
(for
2
-D case,
\tilde{p}(\delta)>2
requires
\delta>\frac{1}{2}
). Hence, (
3.13
), combined with (
3.14
)–(
3.15
), yields the uniform upper bound of
\rho
in
B_{1}
.

3.3.2.
Global
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate for the effective velocity.

With the uniform upper bound of
\rho
at hand, we proceed to control the
L^{\infty}(I)
-bound for
v
. From the evolution equation (
4.14
) and the method of characteristics, we obtain

(3.16)

\|v\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times I)}\leq|v_{0}|_{\infty}+\frac{A\gamma}{2a_{1}\delta}\|\rho^{\gamma-\delta}u\|_{L^{1}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))}\leq C_{0}+C(T)\|\rho^{1-\delta}u\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

To estimate the integral term above, we first consider the
L^{2}(I)
-estimate for
\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}u
. Multiplying
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{2}
by
u
instead of
r^{2}u
and integrating over
I
, we obtain an energy inequality with an extra integral term involving
\frac{\rho vu^{2}}{r}
. This term can be further controlled by
|v|_{\infty}
, yielding

\|\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(I))}+\|\rho^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(I))}\leq C(T)\big(\|v\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times I)}^{2}+1\big),

which, combined with the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,1}(I)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(I)
, yields that, all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

(3.17)

\|\rho^{1-\delta}u\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))}\leq C(\varepsilon,T)\big(1+\|v\|_{L^{1}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))}^{2}\big)+\varepsilon\|v\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times I)}^{2}.

Substituting (
3.17
) into (
3.16
) and choosing
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, the Grönwall inequality gives the desired bound
\|v\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times I)}\leq C(T)
.

3.3.3.
Non-formation of cavitation inside the fluids.

Using the
L^{\infty}
bounds for
\rho
and
v
, we can further show that no vacuum forms inside the fluid in finite time, provided that no vacuum state occurs initially. The crucial step is to derive

(3.18)

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(I))}+\|\rho^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(I))}+\|u\|_{L^{4}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))}\leq C(T).

In fact, once (
3.18
) is established, we can derive from
v=u+\frac{2a_{1}\delta}{\delta-1}(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}
and
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of
v
that
\|(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(0,1))}\leq C(T)
, and obtain from the continuity equation
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{1}
that
\|\rho^{\delta-1}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(0,1))}\leq C(T)
. Hence, the embedding
H^{1}(0,1)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(0,1)
yields
\|\rho^{\delta-1}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}(0,1))}\leq C(T)
, which implies the strictly positive lower bound for
\rho
in
[0,T]\times[0,1]
. Similar estimates can be extended to any bounded interval
[0,R]
with constants depending on
R
, which give the pointwise estimate for
\rho
in
[0,T]\times I
.

To establish (
3.18
), we can first derive from (
3.17
) and the
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of
v
that
\|\rho^{1-\delta}u\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))}\leq C(T)
. Then, based on this, multiplying
\eqref{e1.5hh}_{2}
by
\rho^{-\alpha}u
with
\alpha=\max\{0,\frac{5\delta-3}{2}\}
and integrating over
I
, we obtain an intermediate
L^{5}(I)
-estimate for
\rho^{\frac{1-\alpha}{5}}u
:

\big\|\rho^{\frac{1-\alpha}{5}}u\big\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{5}(I))}+\big\|\rho^{\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}}|u|^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u\big\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(I))}\leq C(T),

which is crucial and further leads to
\|\rho^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}u\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))}\leq C(T)
. Multiplying
\eqref{cosingu}_{2}
by
\rho^{-1}u
and integrating over
I
, together with
L^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))
-bound of
\rho^{\frac{1-\delta}{2}}u
and the
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimates for
(\rho,v)
, give the desired estimate (
3.18
).

3.3.4.
Global
a priori
estimates of the regular solutions

With the
L^{\infty}(I)
-bounds for
(\rho,v)
established, we turn to the enlarged system (
7.2
) to obtain the global estimates for the regular solutions. To simplify the calculations, we provide two auxiliary lemmas (see Lemmas
7.1
–
7.2
in §
7
), indicating the equivalence between the
W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norms and weighted
H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norms of the gradient and the divergence of a spherically symmetric vector function, which are useful in establishing the elliptic estimates for
u
.

Another key step is to obtain the
L^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))
-estimate for
\mathrm{D}_{r}u
. To this end, we establish an inequality of the form:
|r\mathrm{D}_{r}u|_{\infty}\leq C(T)(|ru_{t}|_{2}+1)
via equation
\eqref{e2.2}_{2}
and the Hardy inequality. This allows us to close the first-order temporal estimate for
u
, thereby obtaining
\mathrm{D}_{r}u\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))
. With this estimate at hand, together with Lemmas
7.1
–
7.2
, we can systematically establish the remaining global uniform estimates for
(\phi,u,\psi)
.

4.
Global Uniform Upper Bound of the Density

This section is devoted to establishing the global upper bound of
\rho
. In §
4
–§
8
, we denote
C_{0}\in[1,\infty)
a generic constant depending only on
\left(\rho_{0},u_{0},n,a_{1},A,\gamma,\delta\right)
, and
C\left(\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k}\right)\in[1,\infty)
a generic constant depending on
C_{0}
and parameters
\left(\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k}\right)
, which may be different at each occurrence. Besides, we define the characteristic functions
(\chi^{\flat}_{\sigma},\chi^{\sharp}_{\sigma})
(
\sigma>0
) as

(4.1)

\chi^{\flat}_{\sigma}(r):=\begin{cases}1&\text{for $r\in[0,\sigma)$},\\
0&\text{for $r\in[\sigma,\infty)$},\end{cases}\qquad\ \ \chi^{\sharp}_{\sigma}:=1-\chi^{\flat}_{\sigma}.

We always let

(4.2)

\displaystyle\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1),\ \

\displaystyle\gamma\in(1,\infty)\qquad\,\,

\displaystyle\text{if $n=2$};

\displaystyle\delta\in(7-2\sqrt{10},1),\ \

\displaystyle\gamma\in(1,6\delta-3)\qquad\,\,

\displaystyle\text{if $n=3$}.

4.1.
Reformulation in the Spherical Coordinates

Let
T>0
be any fixed time and let the vector
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
be the unique regular solution of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which has the following form:

(4.3)

\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\rho(t,r),\quad\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=u(t,r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}\qquad\,\text{for $r=|\boldsymbol{x}|$}.

Then the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) can be transformed to the following
IBVP
in
[0,T]\times I
:

(4.4)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+u\rho_{r}+\rho\big(u_{r}+\frac{mu}{r}\big)=0,\\[3.0pt]
\displaystyle\rho u_{t}+\rho uu_{r}+A(\rho^{\gamma})_{r}=2a_{1}\delta\big(\rho^{\delta}u_{r}+\frac{m\rho^{\delta}u}{r}\big)_{r}-\frac{2a_{1}m(\rho^{\delta})_{r}u}{r},\\[3.0pt]
\displaystyle(\rho,u)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},u_{0})\qquad\text{for $r\in I$},\\[3.0pt]
\displaystyle u|_{r=0}=0\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \text{for $t\in(0,T]$},\\[3.0pt]
\displaystyle(\rho,u)\to\left(0,0\right)\qquad\qquad\,\text{as $r\to\infty$ \ for $t\in(0,T]$}.\end{cases}

Next, by Lemma
B.1
in Appendix B, we can transform the statements of Theorems
3.1
–
3.2
into spherical coordinates.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
n=2
or
3
and (
2.2
) hold. Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})(r)
satisfy

(4.5)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}>0,\qquad r^{m}\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(I),\qquad r^{\frac{m}{2n}}(\rho_{0}^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}})_{r}\in L^{2n}(I),

\displaystyle r^{\frac{m}{n}}\mathrm{D}_{r}(\rho_{0}^{\delta-1})_{r}\in L^{n}(I),\qquad r^{\frac{m}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}(\rho_{0}^{\delta-1})_{r}\in L^{2}(I),

\displaystyle r^{\frac{m}{2}}((\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{r},\mathrm{D}_{r}(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{r},\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{r},u_{0},\mathrm{D}_{r}u_{0},\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u_{0},\mathrm{D}_{r}^{3}u_{0})\in L^{2}(I),

and the initial compatibility conditions
:

(4.6)

\mathrm{D}_{r}u_{0}=r^{-\frac{m}{2}}\phi^{-\iota}_{0}(g_{1},g_{2}),\qquad\mathrm{D}_{r}(\phi^{2\iota}_{0}\tilde{L}u_{0})=r^{-\frac{m}{2}}\phi^{-\iota}_{0}(g_{3},g_{4}),\qquad\tilde{L}u_{0}=r^{-\frac{m}{2}}\phi^{-2\iota}_{0}g_{5},

from some functions
g_{i}

(
1\leq i\leq 5
)
belonging to
L^{2}(I)
, where
\tilde{L}u_{0}:=((u_{0})_{r}+\frac{m}{r}u_{0})_{r}
and the operator
\mathrm{D}_{r}
is defined in
§
3.1.2
. Then there exist both
T_{*}>0
and a unique regular solution
(\rho,u)(t,r)
in
[0,T_{*}]\times I
of problem (
4.4
) satisfying

(4.7)

\displaystyle\rho>0,\quad r^{m}\rho\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{1}(I)),\quad(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{\infty}(I)),

\displaystyle r^{\frac{m}{2}}((\rho^{\gamma-1})_{r},\mathrm{D}_{r}(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{r},\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{r})\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(I)),

\displaystyle r^{\frac{m}{n}}\mathrm{D}_{r}(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{n}(I)),\quad r^{\frac{m}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(I)),

\displaystyle r^{\frac{m}{2}}\big(u,\mathrm{D}_{r}u,\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u,\mathrm{D}_{r}^{3}u,u_{t},\mathrm{D}_{r}u_{t}\big)\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(I)),

\displaystyle r^{\frac{m}{2}}\rho^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(I)),\quad r^{\frac{m}{2}}\rho^{\frac{\delta-1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u_{t}\in L^{\infty}([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(I)),

\displaystyle r^{\frac{m}{2}}\rho^{\delta-1}(\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u,\mathrm{D}_{r}^{3}u)\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(I)),\quad r^{\frac{m}{2}}(\mathrm{D}_{r}^{4}u,\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u_{t},\rho^{\delta-1}\mathrm{D}_{r}^{4}u)\in L^{2}([0,T_{*}];L^{2}),

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{2}}r^{\frac{m}{2}}(u_{tt},\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u_{t},\mathrm{D}_{r}^{4}u)\in L^{\infty}([0,T_{*}];L^{2}),\quad t^{\frac{1}{2}}r^{\frac{m}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T_{*}];L^{2}).

In addition, the following regularity properties hold
:

(4.8)

(\rho,\rho_{r},\rho_{t},u,\mathrm{D}_{r}u)\in C([0,T_{*}]\times C(\bar{I})),\qquad(\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u,u_{t})\in C((0,T_{*}]\times C(\bar{I})).

4.2.
Some Basic Estimates and Radial Weighted Estimates for
\rho

First, the so-called effective velocity is defined as follows.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be defined as in
§1
. The effective velocity
v
is defined as

(4.9)

v=u+2a_{1}\delta\rho^{\delta-2}\rho_{r}=u+\frac{2a_{1}\delta}{\delta-1}(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}.

Besides, define
v_{0}:=v|_{t=0}=u_{0}+2a_{1}\delta\rho_{0}^{\delta-2}(\rho_{0})_{r}
.

Then we have the following energy estimates and the BD entropy estimates.

Lemma 4.2
.

For all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}(\rho u^{2}+\rho^{\gamma})(t,r)\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}\rho^{\delta}|\mathrm{D}_{r}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{0},

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}\big(\rho v^{2}+|(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}|^{2}+\rho^{\gamma}\big)(t,r)\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}\rho^{\gamma+\delta-3}\rho_{r}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{0},

where the operator
\mathrm{D}_{r}
is defined in
§
3.1.2
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps.

1.
First, multiplying
\eqref{e1.5}_{2}
by
r^{m}u
, along with
\eqref{e1.5}_{1}
, yields

(4.10)

\displaystyle\big(\frac{1}{2}r^{m}\rho u^{2}+\frac{A}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}\big)_{t}

\displaystyle=-2a_{1}r^{m}\rho^{\delta}\underline{\Big(\delta u_{r}^{2}-2(1-\delta)mu_{r}\frac{u}{r}+(m-(1-\delta)m^{2})\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\Big)}_{:=I_{1}}

\displaystyle+\Big(r^{m}\big(2a_{1}\rho^{\delta}u\big(\delta u_{r}+(\delta-1)m\frac{u}{r}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{3}-\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}u\rho^{\gamma}\big)\Big)_{r}.

Letting
(X,Y)=(u_{r},\frac{u}{r})
, then
I_{1}
becomes a binary form:

I_{1}=\delta X^{2}-2(1-\delta)mXY+(m-(1-\delta)m^{2})Y^{2}

and its discriminant
\mathscr{D}
satisfies

\mathscr{D}=4(1-\delta)^{2}m^{2}-4\delta(m-(1-\delta)m^{2})<0\qquad\text{whenever}\ \ \delta>\frac{m}{m+1}=1-\frac{1}{n}.

Hence, there exists a constant
c^{*}_{\delta}>0
, depending only on
\delta
, such that

I_{1}\geq c^{*}_{\delta}(X^{2}+Y^{2})=c^{*}_{\delta}\big(u_{r}^{2}+\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big),

which, along with (
4.10
), leads to

(4.11)

\displaystyle\big(\frac{1}{2}r^{m}\rho u^{2}+\frac{A}{\gamma-1}r^{m}\rho^{\gamma}\big)_{t}+2a_{1}c^{*}_{\delta}r^{m}\rho^{\delta}\big(u_{r}^{2}+\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(r^{m}\underline{\big(2a_{1}\rho^{\delta}u\big(\delta u_{r}+(\delta-1)m\frac{u}{r}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{3}-\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}u\rho^{\gamma}\big)}_{:=\mathcal{B}_{1}}\Big)_{r}.

Next, we prove
r^{m}\mathrm{D}_{r}\mathcal{B}_{1}\in L^{1}(I)
for
a.e.

t\in(0,T)
, so that Lemma
A.3
can be applied to obtain

(4.12)

\int_{0}^{\infty}(r^{m}\mathcal{B}_{1})_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r=0.

Indeed, it follows from (
4.7
)–(
4.8
) that, for
a.e.

t\in(0,T)
,

r^{m}\rho\in L^{1}(I),\quad(\rho,(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r},u)\in L^{\infty}(I),\quad r^{\frac{m}{2}}(u,\mathrm{D}_{r}u,\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u,(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{r})\in L^{2}(I).

Then we obtain from the above that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |r^{m}\mathrm{D}_{r}\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{1}&\leq C_{0}\big|(r^{m}\rho^{\gamma}\mathrm{D}_{r}u,r^{m}u\rho(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{r})\big|_{1}+C_{0}|r^{m-1}\rho^{\delta}u\mathrm{D}_{r}u|_{1}\\
&\quad+C_{0}\big|\big(r^{m}\rho(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}u\mathrm{D}_{r}u,r^{m}\rho^{\delta}u_{r}\mathrm{D}_{r}u,r^{m}\rho^{\delta}u\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u\big)\big|_{1}\\
&\quad+C_{0}\big|\big(r^{m-1}\rho u^{3},r^{m}\rho^{2-\delta}(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}u^{3},r^{m}\rho u^{2}u_{r}\big)\big|_{1}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\ \ \begin{aligned} &\leq C_{0}\big(|\mathrm{D}_{r}u|_{\infty}|r^{m}\rho|_{1}|\rho|_{\infty}^{\gamma-1}+|\rho|_{\infty}|r^{\frac{m}{2}}(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{r}|_{2}|r^{\frac{m}{2}}u|_{2}\big)\\
&\quad+C_{0}|\rho|_{\infty}^{\delta}\big(|r^{\frac{m}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u|_{2}^{2}+|r^{\frac{m}{2}}u|_{2}|r^{\frac{m}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}^{2}u|_{2}\big)\\
&\quad+C_{0}|\rho|_{\infty}|(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}|_{\infty}|r^{\frac{m}{2}}u|_{2}|r^{\frac{m}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u|_{2}\\
&\quad+C_{0}|r^{m}\rho|_{1}|u|_{\infty}^{2}|\mathrm{D}_{r}u|_{\infty}+C_{0}|\rho|_{\infty}^{2-\delta}|(\rho^{\delta-1})_{r}|_{\infty}|u|_{\infty}|r^{\frac{m}{2}}u|_{2}^{2}<\infty.\end{aligned}

Thus, integrating (
4.11
) over
[0,t]\times I
, along with (
4.12
), gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(\frac{1}{2}r^{m}\rho u^{2}+\frac{A}{\gamma-1}r^{m}\rho^{\gamma}\big)(t,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}r+2a_{1}c^{*}_{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}\rho^{\delta}|\mathrm{D}_{r}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\big(|(r^{m}\rho_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{0}|_{2}^{2}+|r^{\frac{m}{\gamma}}\rho_{0}|_{\gamma}^{\gamma}\big),

where the initial data can be handled by Lemmas
A.1
,
A.10
, and
B.1
:

\displaystyle|(r^{m}\rho_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{0}|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}|r^{m}\rho_{0}|_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}|u_{0}|_{\infty}\leq C_{0}\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{0},

\displaystyle|r^{\frac{m}{\gamma}}\rho_{0}|_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}|r^{m}\rho_{0}|_{1}|\rho_{0}|_{\infty}^{\gamma-1}\leq C_{0}\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\gamma-1}\leq C_{0}.

2.
Multiplying
\eqref{e1.5}_{1}
by
2a_{1}\delta\rho^{\delta-1}
and applying
\partial_{r}
to the resulting equality give

\displaystyle 2a_{1}\delta\rho(\rho^{\delta-2}\rho_{r})_{t}+2a_{1}\delta\rho u(\rho^{\delta-2}\rho_{r})_{r}+2a_{1}\delta\big(\rho^{\delta}(u_{r}+\frac{mu}{r})\big)_{r}-\frac{2a_{1}m(\rho^{\delta})_{r}u}{r}=0,

which, together with
\eqref{e1.5}_{2}
, leads to

(4.13)

\rho(v_{t}+uv_{r})+A(\rho^{\gamma})_{r}=0.

Then, multiplying (
4.13
) by
r^{m}v
and
integrating the resulting equality over
[0,t]\times I
, we derive from
\eqref{e1.5}_{1}
and the similar argument in Step 1 that

\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}(\rho v^{2}+\rho^{\gamma})(t,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}\rho^{\gamma+\delta-3}\rho_{r}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{0}\big(|(r^{m}\rho_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{0}|_{2}^{2}+1\big).

For the
L^{2}
-boundedness of
(r^{m}\rho_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{0}
, it follows from Lemmas
A.1
,
A.10
, and
B.1
that

|(r^{m}\rho_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{0}|_{2}\leq C_{0}|r^{m}\rho_{0}|_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}|(u_{0},(\rho_{0}^{\delta-1})_{r})|_{\infty}\leq C_{0}\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\nabla(\rho_{0}^{\delta-1}))\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{0}.

Finally, the
L^{2}
-estimate of
r^{\frac{m}{2}}(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}
follows directly from the
L^{2}
-estimates of
(r^{m}\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)
and (
4.9
). This completes the proof.
∎

Clearly, by (
4.9
) and (
4.13
), we have

Corollary 4.1
.

The effective velocity
v
satisfies the following equation
:

(4.14)

v_{t}+uv_{r}+\frac{A\gamma}{2a_{1}\delta}\rho^{\gamma-\delta}(v-u)=0.

Next, we establish the
L^{p}
-estimates,
p\in[1,\infty]
, of
r^{m}\rho
away from the origin.

Lemma 4.3
.

For any
\omega>0
, there exists a constant
C(\omega)>0
such that

\displaystyle|r^{m}\rho(t)|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}

\displaystyle\text{for all $t\in[0,T]$},

\displaystyle|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m}\rho(t)|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq C(\omega)

\displaystyle\text{for all $p\in(1,\infty]$ and $t\in[0,T]$}.

Proof.

First, integrating
\eqref{e1.5}_{1}
over
I
, we obtain
from Lemma
A.3
and
r^{m}\mathrm{D}_{r}(\rho u)\in L^{1}(I)
for
a.e.

t\in(0,T)
due to (
4.7
)–(
4.8
) that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{m}\rho\,\mathrm{d}r=-\int_{0}^{\infty}(r^{m}\rho u)_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r=r^{m}\rho u|_{r=0}=0.

Then integrating the above over
[0,t]
yields

(4.15)

|r^{m}\rho(t)|_{1}=|r^{m}\rho_{0}|_{1}\leq C_{0}\qquad\text{for any $t\in[0,T]$}.

Next, let
\omega>0
. It follows from (
4.15
), Lemmas
4.2
and
A.3
, and the Hölder inequality that, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m}\rho|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}(r^{m}\rho)_{r}|_{1}\leq C_{0}\big(|\chi_{\omega}^{\sharp}r^{m}\rho^{\frac{3}{2}-\delta}(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}|_{1}+|\chi_{\omega}^{\sharp}r^{m-1}\rho|_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\big(|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}\rho|_{\infty}^{1-\delta}|r^{m}\rho|_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}|r^{\frac{m}{2}}(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}|_{2}+|\chi_{\omega}^{\sharp}r^{-1}|_{\infty}|r^{m}\rho|_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m(\delta-1)}|_{\infty}|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m}\rho|_{\infty}^{1-\delta}+C(\omega)\leq C(\omega)\big(|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m}\rho|_{\infty}^{1-\delta}+1\big),

which, along with the Young inequality, gives
|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m}\rho|_{\infty}\leq C(\omega)
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Finally, it follows from the above and (
4.15
) that,
for any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
t\in[0,T]
,

|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m}\rho|_{p}\leq|\chi^{\sharp}_{\omega}r^{m}\rho|_{\infty}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}|r^{m}\rho|_{1}^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C(\omega),

where
C(\omega)\in[1,\infty)
is a generic constant depending on
C_{0}
and
\omega
, which may be different at each occurrence. This completes the proof.
∎

The next lemma concerns the
r
-weighted
L^{p}
-estimates (
p\in[1,\infty]
) for
\rho
near the origin.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
t\in[0,T]
,

\mathrm{(i)}

if
n=2
, there exist two positive constants
C(\nu,p)
and
C(\nu)
such that

\displaystyle|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\nu}\rho(t)|_{p}\leq C(p,\nu)

\displaystyle\text{for all $\nu>-\frac{1}{p}$ and $p\in(0,\infty)$},

\displaystyle|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\nu}\rho(t)|_{\infty}\leq C(\nu)

\displaystyle\text{for all $\nu>0$};

\mathrm{(ii)}

if
n=3
, there exists two positive constants
C(p)
and
C_{0}
such that

\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\frac{1}{2\delta-1}-\frac{1}{p}}\rho(t)\big|_{p}\leq C(p)\quad\text{for all $p\in[2\delta-1,\infty)$},\qquad\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\frac{1}{2\delta-1}}\rho(t)\big|_{\infty}\leq C_{0}.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps.

1.
By Lemmas
4.2
–
4.3
and
A.4

and the Hölder inequality, we obtain that, for all
\nu>0
,

\displaystyle|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\nu}\rho|_{\infty}^{2\delta-1}

\displaystyle=|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\nu(\delta-\frac{1}{2})}\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}}|_{\infty}^{2}\leq C(\nu)\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\nu(\delta-\frac{1}{2})+\frac{1}{2}}(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}},(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r})\big|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(\nu)|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r\rho^{2\delta-1}|_{1}+C(\nu)|r^{\frac{1}{2}}(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(\nu)|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r|_{1}^{2-2\delta}|r\rho|_{1}^{2\delta-1}+C(\nu)\leq C(\nu).

Next, for all
p\in(0,\infty)
and
\nu>-\frac{1}{p}
, we can fix
\varepsilon
such that
0<\varepsilon<\min\{p\nu+1,1\}
, and then obtain

|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\nu}\rho|_{p}^{p}=\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{p\nu}\rho^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{p\nu-\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\frac{\varepsilon}{p}}\rho|_{\infty}^{p}\leq C(p,\nu).

This completes the proof of (i).

2.
For any
p\in[2\delta-1,\infty)
, it follows from Lemmas
4.2
–
4.3
and
A.4
and the Hölder inequality that

\displaystyle|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\frac{1}{2\delta-1}-\frac{1}{p}}\rho|_{p}

\displaystyle=\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\frac{p-2\delta+1}{2p}}\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\frac{2p}{2\delta-1}}^{\frac{2}{2\delta-1}}\leq C(p)\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}},(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r})\big|_{2}^{\frac{2}{2\delta-1}}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\big(|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{2}|_{1}^{\frac{2-2\delta}{2\delta-1}}|r^{2}\rho|_{1}+\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r}\big|_{2}^{\frac{2}{2\delta-1}}\big)\leq C(p).

Similarly, we can also obtain

|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\frac{1}{2\delta-1}}\rho|_{\infty}=\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\infty}^{\frac{2}{2\delta-1}}\leq C_{0}\big|\chi_{1}^{\flat}r(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}},(\rho^{\delta-\frac{1}{2}})_{r})\big|_{2}^{\frac{2}{2\delta-1}}\leq C_{0}.

This completes the proof of (ii).
∎

4.3.
L^{p}(I)
-Energy Estimates for
(u,v)

We aim to establish the following
L^{p}(I)
-estimates for
(r^{m}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)
:

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\tilde{p}_{m}(\delta)>2
be a special parameter defined by

\tilde{p}_{m}(\delta):=\frac{2\delta(m\delta-(m-1))+2\sqrt{\delta(m\delta-(m-1))((m+1)\delta-m)}}{m(1-\delta)^{2}}.

Then, for any
p\in[2,\tilde{p}_{m}(\delta))
, there exists a constant
C(p,T)
such that, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\big|(r^{m}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)(t)\big|_{p}^{p}+\int_{0}^{t}\big(\big|(r^{m}\rho^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}|u|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{r}u\big|_{2}^{2}+\big|(r^{m}\rho^{\gamma-\delta+1})^{\frac{1}{p}}v\big|_{p}^{p}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C(p,T).

The proof of Lemma
4.5
is divided into the following several lemmas. First, we prove that
\tilde{p}_{m}(\delta)>n
if
\delta
satisfies conditions (
4.2
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\tilde{p}_{m}(\delta)
be defined as in
Lemma
4.5
. Then
\tilde{p}_{m}(\delta)
is the larger root of the following quadratic equation with respect to
p
:

m^{2}(\delta-1)^{2}p^{2}-4\delta(\delta m^{2}-m^{2}+m)p+4\delta(\delta m^{2}-m^{2}+m)=0.

In particular, the following properties hold
:

\displaystyle 2<\tilde{p}_{1}(\delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\delta^{2}+2\delta\sqrt{2\delta-1}}{(\delta-1)^{2}}\quad

\displaystyle\text{if $m=1$ and $\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)$};

\displaystyle 3<\tilde{p}_{2}(\delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta(2\delta-1)+\sqrt{\delta(2\delta-1)(3\delta-2)}}{(\delta-1)^{2}}\quad

\displaystyle\text{if $m=2$ and $\delta\in(7-2\sqrt{10},1)$}.

Proof.

The first assertion follows directly from the formula for the roots of quadratic equations.

Next, if
m=1
, notice that

\tilde{p}_{1}(\delta)>2\iff\delta\sqrt{2\delta-1}+(2\delta-1)>0,

which automatically holds for
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.

Finally, if
m=2
, then

(4.16)

\tilde{p}_{2}(\delta)>3\iff\sqrt{\delta(2\delta-1)(3\delta-2)}>\delta^{2}-5\delta+3.

If
\delta^{2}-5\delta+3\leq 0
, we have

\frac{5-\sqrt{13}}{2}\leq\delta<1,

so that the right-hand side of (
4.16
) automatically holds;
while, if
\delta^{2}-5\delta+3>0
,
